An invariant of symmetry protected topological phases with on-site finite group symmetry for two-dimensional Fermion systems
Yoshiko Ogata

Abstract
We consider SPT-phases with on-site finite group G G symmetry for two-dimensional Fermion systems. We derive an invariant of the classification.



1
Introduction

The notion of symmetry protected topological (SPT) phases was introduced by Gu and Wen [GW].
It is defined as follows:
we consider the set of all Hamiltonians with some symmetry,
which have a unique gapped ground state in the bulk, and can be smoothly deformed into
a common trivial gapped Hamiltonian without closing the gap.
We say
two such Hamiltonians are equivalent, if they can be smoothly deformed into
each other, without breaking the symmetry.
We call an equivalence class of this classification, a
symmetry protected topological (SPT) phase.
In
[
BO
]
we derived
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times H^{1}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1))\times H^{2}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1))
-valued
invariant of one-dimensional Fermionic SPT-phases.
In this paper, we derive an invariant of SPT-phases in two-dimensional Fermionic systems.

We start by summarizing standard setup of Fermionic systems on the two dimensional lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

[
BR1
,
BR2
,
EK
]
. We will use freely the basic notation in section
A
.
We also use the facts and notation
of graded
C^{*}
-algebra from
[
Bla
]
and
[
BO
]
.

Let us first recall the definition of the self-dual CAR-algebra introduced by Araki
[
A
]
.
(See also
[
EK
]
Chapter 6.)

Definition 1.1
.

For a Hilbert space
{\mathfrak{K}}
with a complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}
(i.e., anti-unitary such that
{\mathfrak{C}}={\mathfrak{C}}^{*}
),
self-dual-CAR-algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})

over
({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})

is defined as the universal enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra
generated by
\{B(f)\mid f\in{\mathfrak{K}}\}

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathfrak{K}}\ni f\mapsto B(f),\quad\text{linear}\\
\left\{B(f),B(g)\right\}=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle\mathbb{I},\\
B(f)^{*}=B({\mathfrak{C}}f),\quad f,g\in{\mathfrak{K}}.\end{split}

(1.1)

If
u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathfrak{K}})
satisfies
u{\mathfrak{C}}={\mathfrak{C}}u
, then
there exists an automorphism
\Xi_{u}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}}))

such that
\Xi_{u}\left(B(f)\right)=B\left(uf\right)
,
f\in{\mathfrak{K}}
.
In particular, for
u=-\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{K}}
,

\Theta_{\mathfrak{K}}:=\Xi_{-\mathbb{I}}
defines an automorphism on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})

satisfying
\Theta_{\mathfrak{K}}^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. Hence it defines a grading over
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
.
In general, for a graded
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathfrak{B}}
, we denote by
{\mathfrak{B}}^{(0)}

its even part and by
{\mathfrak{B}}^{(1)}

its odd part. Elements in
{\mathfrak{B}}^{(0)}
or
{\mathfrak{B}}^{(1)}
are
said to be homogeneous.
For a homogeneous element
b\in{\mathfrak{B}}
,
we denote by
\partial b
the grading of
b
.
With this grading,
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
has an odd self-adjoint unitary
(consider
B(f)
for
f\in{\mathfrak{K}}
with
{\mathfrak{C}}f=f
,
\left\|f\right\|^{2}=2
.)
We say a state
\omega
on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
is homogeneous if it is invariant under
\Theta_{\mathfrak{K}}
.
When an automorphism
\alpha
on
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}}))
commutes with
\Theta_{\mathfrak{K}}
,
we say that
\alpha
is graded. We denote by
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})\right)

the set of all graded automorphisms on
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}}))
.

A basis projection for
({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
is an orthogonal projection
on
{\mathfrak{K}}
such that
p+{\mathfrak{C}}p{\mathfrak{C}}=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{K}}
.
Basis projection exists if
{\mathfrak{K}}
is even or infinite dimensional.
If
p
is a basis projection for
({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
,
the self-dual CAR-algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
is
*
-isomorphic to the CAR-algebra

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rm CAR}(p{\mathfrak{K}})
over
p{\mathfrak{K}}

via a
*
-isomorphism
\gamma_{p}:{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})\to{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rm CAR}(p{\mathfrak{K}})

such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}\left(B(f)\right)=a^{*}(pf)+a(p{\mathfrak{C}}f),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{K}}.

(1.2)

Here,
a^{*}(g),a(g)
,
g\in p{\mathfrak{K}}
denotes the creation and anihilation operators of the CAR-algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rm CAR}(p{\mathfrak{K}})
.
With the Fock state
\omega
over
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rm CAR}(p{\mathfrak{K}})
(i.e., the state with
\omega(a^{*}(g)a(g))=0
for any

g\in p{\mathfrak{K}}
), via the
*
-isomorphism above,
we can define a state
\omega_{p}:=\omega\gamma_{p}
over
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}})
.

For Hilbert spaces
{\mathfrak{K}}_{1},

{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}
with complex conjugations
{\mathfrak{C}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}
,
there is a
*
-isomorphism

\gamma_{12}:{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1})\hat{\otimes}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2})\to{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)

such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{12}\left(B_{1}(f_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{I}\hat{\otimes}B_{2}(f_{2})\right)=B(f_{1}\oplus f_{2}),\quad f_{1}\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{1},\;f_{2}\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}.

(1.3)

(Recall the graded tensor product
\hat{\otimes}
from
[
Bla
]
section 14.)
Here we denoted the generators of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})
by
B_{i}(f_{i})
,
f_{i}\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}
.
We identify
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1})\hat{\otimes}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2})
and

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
via this isomorphism
throughout this paper, without writing

\gamma_{12}
explicitly.
In particular, for homogeneous states
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1})
,

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2})
,
we denote the state
\left(\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}\right)\circ\gamma_{12}^{-1}

simply by
\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}
.
(Recall that
\hat{\varphi}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\varphi}_{2}
is a state on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1})\hat{\otimes}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2})

such that
(\hat{\varphi}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\varphi}_{2})(a\hat{\otimes}b)=\varphi_{1}(a)\varphi_{2}(b)
.
)
For graded automorphisms
\alpha_{1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1})\right)
,

\alpha_{2}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2})\right)
,
we denote the automorphism
\gamma_{12}\left(\alpha_{1}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{2}\right)\gamma_{12}^{-1}

simply by
\alpha_{1}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{2}
,

Throughout this paper, we fix some
d\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{U}^{k}:={\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq k},\quad H_{D}^{k}:={\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq k},\quad H_{L}^{k}:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq k}\times{\mathbb{Z}},\quad H_{R}^{k}:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq k}\times{\mathbb{Z}}.\end{split}

(1.4)

In particular,
left, right, upper, lower half planes are denoted by
H_{L}:=H_{L}^{-1}
,
H_{R}:=H_{R}^{0}
,

H^{U}:=H_{U}^{0}
,
H_{D}:=H_{D}^{-1}
.
We set
{\mathfrak{h}}:=l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})
.
Let
\{\delta_{(x,y)}\mid(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\}
be the standard basis of
l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})
,
and
\mathfrak{C}
, the complex conjugation on
l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})

with respect to it.

For each
X\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, we set

\displaystyle\hat{X}:=\left\{(dx+j,y)\mid(x,y)\in X,\;j=0,\ldots,d-1\right\},\quad\mathfrak{h}_{X}:=l^{2}(\hat{X})\subset l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}).

(1.5)

We denote the restriction
\mathfrak{C}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{X}}

of
\mathfrak{C}
on
\mathfrak{h}_{X}

by
\mathfrak{C}_{X}
,
and set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{X}:=\mathfrak{A}_{\rm SDC}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{X},\mathfrak{C}_{X}\right),\quad\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{X}:={\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left(\ l^{2}(X),{\mathfrak{C}}|_{l^{2}(X)}\right).

(1.6)

In particular, we set
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
.
We denote by
\Theta_{X}:=\Theta_{\mathfrak{h}_{X}}
the grading automorphism
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}
.
For
X=H_{L},H_{R}
, we also set
\Theta_{L}:=\Theta_{H_{L}}
,

\Theta_{R}:=\Theta_{H_{R}}
.

Now we define a reference state.
For each
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,

\{\delta_{(z,y)}\mid z=dx,dx+1,\ldots,dx+d-1\}

is a CONS of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}:={\mathfrak{h}}_{\{(x,y)\}}
.
One can decompose
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}
as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}

(1.7)

with its
2
-dimensional subspaces

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}:={\mathbb{C}}-\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\nolimits\left\{\delta_{(dx+2j,y)},\delta_{(dx+2j+1,y)}\right\},\quad j=0,\ldots,\frac{d}{2}-1.

(1.8)

Because each
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}
is invariant under
{\mathfrak{C}}
,
its restriction
{\mathfrak{C}}^{(j)}_{(x,y)}:={\mathfrak{C}}|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}}
gives
a complex conjugation
on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}
.

Let
p^{(j)}_{(x,y)}

be the orthogonal projection on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}

onto
the one-dimensional subspace
spanned by
\delta_{(dx+2j,y)}+i\delta_{(dx+2j+1,y)}
.
By the definition, we see
that
{\mathfrak{C}}^{(j)}_{(x,y)}
is a basis projection for

({\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)},{\mathfrak{C}}^{(j)}_{(x,y)})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{C}}^{(j)}_{(x,y)}p^{(j)}_{(x,y)}{\mathfrak{C}}^{(j)}_{(x,y)}=\mathbb{I}_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}^{(j)}}-p^{(j)}_{(x,y)}.

(1.9)

Set projections
p_{(x,y)}
,
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,

p_{X}
,
X\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
on

{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{X}
respectively by

\displaystyle p_{(x,y)}:=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}p_{(x,y)}^{(j)},\quad p_{X}:=\bigoplus_{(x,y)\in X}p_{(x,y)}.

(1.10)

By (
1.9
),
we see that
p_{X}
is a basis projection for
({\mathfrak{h}}_{X},{\mathfrak{C}}_{X})

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{C}}_{X}p_{X}{\mathfrak{C}}_{X}=\mathbb{I}_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{X}}-p_{X}.

(1.11)

In particular,
p:=p_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
is a basis projection
for
({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{C}})
.
From this basis projection
p
, we can
construct a Fock state
\omega^{(0)}:=\omega_{p}

on
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Set
g_{j,(x,y)}:=\delta_{(dx+2j,y)}+i\delta_{(dx+2j+1,y)}
,
for
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and
j=0,\ldots,\frac{d}{2}-1
.
With
\gamma_{p}
in (
1.2
),
we note that

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}^{-1}\left(a^{*}(pg_{j,(x,y)})a(pg_{j,(x,y)})\right)=B(g_{j,(x,y)})B(g_{j,(x,y)})^{*}.

(1.12)

This
\omega^{(0)}
is our reference state.
We also define Fock states on
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{L}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}

out of
basis projections
p_{H_{L}}
,
p_{H_{R}}
on

{\mathfrak{h}}_{H_{L}}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{H_{R}}
,

\omega_{L}:=\omega_{p_{H_{L}}}
,
\omega_{R}:=\omega_{p_{H_{R}}}
.
From the structure we
see that

\displaystyle\omega^{(0)}=\omega_{L}\hat{\otimes}\omega_{R}.

(1.13)

Throughout this paper, we fix a finite group
G

and a unitary representation
U

on
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
,
commuting with the complex conjugation
with respect to the standard basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.
Identifying the standard basis of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{(x,y)}

\{\delta_{(dx+k,y)}\mid k=0,\ldots,d-1\}

with that of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
,
we write the copy of
U

on
\mathfrak{h}_{(x,y)}
as
U_{(x,y)}
.
Note that
U_{(x,y)}(g)
,
g\in G
and
{\mathfrak{C}}_{(x,y)}

commute,
hence
U_{X}(g):=\bigoplus_{(x,y)\in X}U_{(x,y)}(g)
,
g\in G

and
{\mathfrak{C}}_{X}

commute, for each
X\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
As a result, for each
X\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

and
g\in G
there is an automorphism
\beta_{g}^{X}:=\Xi_{U_{X}(g)}
such that

\displaystyle\beta_{g}^{X}\left(B(f)\right)=B\left(U_{X}(g)f\right),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{X}.

(1.14)

We set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{g}:=\beta_{g}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}},\quad\beta_{g}^{U}:=\beta_{g}^{H_{U}^{1}},\quad\beta_{g}^{D}:=\beta_{g}^{H_{D}^{1}},\quad\beta_{g}^{UR}:=\beta_{f}^{H_{U}^{1}\cap H_{R}},\quad\beta_{g}^{UL}:=\beta_{f}^{H_{U}^{1}\cap H_{L}}\quad g\in G.\end{split}

(1.15)

A mathematical model on two dimensional Fermionic system is fully specified by its
even interaction
\Phi
.
We denote the set of all finite subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
by
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
.
A uniformly bounded even interaction on
{\mathcal{A}}

is a map
\Phi:{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}
such that

\displaystyle\Phi(X)=\Phi(X)^{*}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{X}^{(0)},\quad X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}},

(1.16)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\left\|\Phi(X)\right\|<\infty.

(1.17)

It is of finite range with interaction length less than or equal to
R\in\mathbb{N}
if

\Phi(X)=0
for any
X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}

whose diameter is larger than
R
.
An on-site interaction, i.e., an interaction with

\Phi(X)=0
unless
X
consists of a single point, is said to be trivial.
An even interaction
\Phi
is
\beta
-invariant if
\beta_{g}(\Phi(X))=\Phi(X)

for any
X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
.
For a uniformly bounded and finite range even interaction
\Phi
and
\Lambda\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}

define the local Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\left(H_{\Phi}\right)_{\Lambda}:=\sum_{X\subset\Lambda}\Phi(X),

(1.18)

and denote the dynamics

\displaystyle\tau^{(\Lambda)\Phi}_{t}(A):=e^{it\left(H_{\Phi}\right)_{\Lambda}}Ae^{-it\left(H_{\Phi}\right)_{\Lambda}},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad A\in{\mathcal{A}}.

(1.19)

By the uniform boundedness and finite rangeness of
\Phi
,
for each
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the following limit exists

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\tau^{(\Lambda),\Phi}_{t}\left(A\right)=:\tau^{\Phi}_{t}\left(A\right),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

(1.20)

and defines the dynamics
\tau^{\Phi}
on
{\mathcal{A}}
.
For a uniformly bounded and finite range even interaction
\Phi
,
a state
\varphi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
is called a
\tau^{\Phi}
-ground
state
if the inequality

-i\,\varphi(A^{*}{\delta_{\Phi}}(A))\geq 0

holds
for any element
A
in the domain
{\mathcal{D}}({\delta_{\Phi}})
of the generator
{\delta_{\Phi}}
.
Let
\varphi
be a
\tau^{\Phi}
-ground state, with a GNS triple
({\mathcal{H}}_{\varphi},\pi_{\varphi},\Omega_{\varphi})
.
Then there exists a unique positive operator
H_{\varphi,\Phi}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varphi}
such that

e^{itH_{\varphi,\Phi}}\pi_{\varphi}(A)\Omega_{\varphi}=\pi_{\varphi}(\tau^{t}_{\Phi}(A))\Omega_{\varphi}
,
for all
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We call this
H_{\varphi,\Phi}
the bulk Hamiltonian associated with
\varphi
.

Definition 1.2
.

We say that an interaction
\Phi
has a unique gapped ground state if
(i) the
\tau^{\Phi}
-ground state, which we denote as
\omega_{\Phi}
, is unique, and
(ii) there exists a
\gamma>0
such that

\sigma(H_{\omega_{\Phi},\Phi})\setminus\{0\}\subset[\gamma,\infty)
, where
\sigma(H_{\omega_{\Phi},\Phi})
is the spectrum of
H_{\omega_{\Phi},\Phi}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
the set of all uniformly bounded finite range even
interactions, with unique gapped ground state.
We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}
the set of all uniformly bounded finite range

\beta
-invariant
even interactions, with unique gapped ground state.

Set
\Phi_{p}:\mathfrak{S}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(\{(x,y)\}):=\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}B(g_{j,(x,y)})B(g_{j,(x,y)})^{*}-B(g_{j,(x,y)})^{*}B(g_{j,(x,y)}),\quad(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

(1.21)

and
\Phi_{p}(X)=0
otherwise.
Then we see that
\Phi_{p}
is a uniformly bounded on-site
interaction with a unique gapped ground state

\omega^{(0)}:=\omega_{p}

[
A
]
.

In this paper we consider a classification problem of a
subset of
{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}
.
To describe this subset we need
to explain the classification problem of unique gapped ground state phases, without symmetry.
For
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, we denote by
\Pi_{\Gamma}:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
the conditional expectation with respect to the trace state (see Theorem 4.7
[
AM
]
).
Note from the proof of Theorem 4.7
[
AM
]

that
\Pi_{\Gamma}(a\hat{\otimes}b)=0

for any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
and
b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma^{c}}^{(1)}
.
Let
f:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be a continuous decreasing function
with
\lim_{t\to\infty}f(t)=0
.
For each
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
, let

\displaystyle\left\|A\right\|_{f}:=\left\|A\right\|+\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\frac{\left\|A-\Pi_{\Lambda_{N}}(A)\right\|}{f(N)}\right).

(1.22)

We denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{f}
the set of all
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
such that

\left\|A\right\|_{f}<\infty
.
Here
\Lambda_{N}:=[-N,N]^{\times 2}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

The classification of unique gapped ground state phases
{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
without symmetry is the following.

Definition 1.3
.

Two interactions
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
are equivalent if there is
a path of even interactions

\Phi:[0,1]\to{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}

satisfying the following:

1.

\Phi(0)=\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi(1)=\Phi_{1}
.

2.

For each
X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, the map

[0,1]\ni s\to\Phi(X;s)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{X}^{(0)}
is
C^{1}
.
We denote by
\dot{\Phi}(X;s)

the corresponding derivatives.
The interaction obtained by differentiation is denoted by
\dot{\Phi}(s)
, for each
s\in[0,1]
.

3.

There is a number
R\in\mathbb{N}

such that
X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}\nolimits{X}\geq R
imply
\Phi(X;s)=0
, for all
s\in[0,1]
.

4.

Interactions are bounded as follows

\displaystyle C_{b}^{\Phi}:=\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\sup_{X\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\left(\left\|\Phi\left(X;s\right)\right\|+|X|\left\|\dot{\Phi}\left(X;s\right)\right\|\right)<\infty.

(1.23)

5.

Setting

\displaystyle b(\varepsilon):=\sup_{Z\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\sup_{s,s_{0}\in[0,1],0<|s-s_{0}|<\varepsilon}\left\|\frac{\Phi(Z;s)-\Phi(Z;s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}-\dot{\Phi}(Z;s_{0})\right\|

(1.24)

for each
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}b(\varepsilon)=0
.

6.

There exists a
\gamma>0
such that

\sigma(H_{\omega_{\Phi(s)},\Phi(s)})\setminus\{0\}\subset[\gamma,\infty)
for
all
s\in[0,1]
, where
\sigma(H_{\omega_{\Phi(s)},\Phi(s)})
is the spectrum of
H_{\omega_{\Phi(s)},\Phi(s)}
.

7.

There exists an
0<\eta<1
satisfying the following:
Set
\zeta(t):=e^{-t^{\eta}}
.
Then for each
A\in D_{\zeta}
,

\omega_{\Phi(s)}(A)
is differentiable with respect to
s
, and there is a constant

C_{\zeta}
such that:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d}{ds}\omega_{\Phi(s)}(A)\right|\leq C_{\zeta}\left\|A\right\|_{\zeta},

(1.25)

for any
A\in D_{\zeta}
.(Recall (
1.22
)).

We write
\Phi_{0}\sim\Phi_{1}
if

\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
are equivalent.
If
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}
and
if we can take the path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}
, i.e.,
so that
\beta_{g}\left(\Phi(X;s)\right)=\Phi(X;s)
,
g\in G
for all
s\in[0,1]
,
then we say
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
are
\beta
-equivalent
and write
\Phi_{0}\sim_{\beta}\Phi_{1}
.

The object we classify in this paper is the following:

Definition 1.4
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{SL\beta}
the set of all

\Phi\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}

such that
\Phi\sim\Phi_{p}

with
\Phi_{p}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
defined in (
1.21
).
Connected components of

{\mathcal{P}}_{SL\beta}
with respect to

\sim_{\beta}
are the
symmetry protected topological (SPT)-phases.

In this paper, we introduce an invariant of
this classification.

2
Main result

Our invariant is
given by

\displaystyle\left(C^{3}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))\right)\times\left(H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\right)\times\left(H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\right),

(2.1)

devided by some equivalence relation.
First let us specify it.

For
A:={\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, we define a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-action on
A\oplus A

by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times\left(A\oplus A\right)\ni(a,x)\mapsto x^{a}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}^{a}x\in A\oplus A.

(2.2)

We associate
A\oplus A
the point-wise multiplication , i.e.,
for
x=(x_{+},x_{-}),y=(y_{+},y_{-})\in A\oplus A
, we set
x\cdot y:=(x_{+}y_{+},x_{-}y_{-})
.
For
x=(x_{+},x_{-})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, we also
set
(-1)^{x}:=((-1)^{x_{+}},(-1)^{x_{-}})\in{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.
For
x\in C^{1}(G,A\oplus A)
,
y\in C^{2}(G,A\oplus A)
,
z\in C^{3}(G,A\oplus A)
and
a\in H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
,
we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&d_{a}^{1}x(g,h):=\frac{\left(x^{a(g)}(h)\right)\cdot x(g)}{x(gh)},\\
&d_{a}^{2}y(g,h,k):=\frac{\left(y^{a(g)}(h,k)\right)\cdot y(g,hk)}{y(gh,k)\cdot y(g,h)},\\
&d_{a}^{3}z(g,h,k,f):=\frac{\left(\left(z^{a(g)}(h,k,f)\right)\right)\cdot z(g,hk,f)\cdot z(g,h,k)}{z(gh,k,f)\cdot z(g,h,kf)}.\end{split}

(2.3)

We use the following Lemma below. The proof is straightforward from the definition.

Lemma 2.1
.

For any
m,x\in C^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
,
a\in H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
setting
\tilde{\sigma}(g,h):=(-1)^{x(g)\cdot m^{a(g)}(h)}
,
we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{d^{1}_{a}x(g,h)\cdot m^{a(gh)}(k)+x(g)\cdot d_{a}^{1}m^{a(g)(h,k)}}=d^{2}_{a}\tilde{\sigma}(g,h,k).

(2.4)

We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}(G)}
the pentad
(c,\kappa_{R},\kappa_{L},b,a)

of

\displaystyle c\in C^{3}\left(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1)\right),\;\kappa_{R}\in C^{2}\left(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\right),\;\kappa_{L}\in C^{2}\left(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\right),\;b\in C^{1}\left(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\right),\;a\in H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})

(2.5)

such that

\displaystyle d_{a}^{1}b(g,h)=\kappa_{L}(g,h)+\kappa_{R}(g,h),

(2.6)

\displaystyle d_{a}^{2}\kappa_{R}(g,h,k)=0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle d_{a}^{2}\kappa_{L}(g,h,k)=0,

(2.8)

\displaystyle d_{a}^{3}c(g,h,k,f)=(-1)^{\kappa_{L}(g,h)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{a(gh)}(k,f)\right)}.

(2.9)

Lemma 2.2
.

On
\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)
,
set

(c^{(1)},\kappa_{R}^{(1)},\kappa_{L}^{(1)},b^{(1)},a^{(1)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(c^{(2)},\kappa_{R}^{(2)},\kappa_{L}^{(2)},b^{(2)},a^{(2)})

for
(c^{(1)},\kappa_{R}^{(1)},\kappa_{L}^{(1)},b^{(1)},a^{(1)}),(c^{(2)},\kappa_{R}^{(2)},\kappa_{L}^{(2)},b^{(2)},a^{(2)})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)

if
the following hold.

(i)

a^{(1)}(g)=a^{(2)}(g)=:a(g)
for any
g\in G
, and

(ii)

there exist an
m\in C^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})

and a
\sigma\in C^{2}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))

such that

\displaystyle\kappa_{R}^{(2)}(g,h)=d_{a}^{1}m(g,h)+\kappa_{R}^{(1)}(g,h),

(2.10)

\displaystyle\kappa_{L}^{(2)}(g,h)=d_{a}^{1}b^{(2)}(g,h)-d_{a}^{1}b^{(1)}(g,h)-d_{a}^{1}m(g,h)+\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h),

(2.11)

\displaystyle c^{(2)}(g,h,k)=\left(-1\right)^{\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h)\cdot m^{a(gh)}(k)}\left(-1\right)^{\left(b^{(2)}(g)-b^{(1)}(g)-m(g)\right)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{(2)}\right)^{a(g)}(h,k)}d_{a}^{2}\sigma(g,h,k)c^{(1)}(g,h,k).

(2.12)

Then this
\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}
is an equivalence relation.

Definition 2.3
.

We denote the equivalence classes by
\mathcal{PD}(G)
.
We also denote by
[(c,\kappa_{R},\kappa_{L},b,a)]_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}

the equivalence class containing
(c,\kappa_{R},\kappa_{L},b,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)
.

We show only the transitivity.
The proof for the symmetry is analogous.
Suppose that

\displaystyle(c^{(1)},\kappa_{R}^{(1)},\kappa_{L}^{(1)},b^{(1)},a^{(1)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(c^{(2)},\kappa_{R}^{(2)},\kappa_{L}^{(2)},b^{(2)},a^{(2)})

with
m\in C^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})

and a
\sigma\in C^{2}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))
, and

\displaystyle(c^{(2)},\kappa_{R}^{(2)},\kappa_{L}^{(2)},b^{(2)},a^{(2)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(c^{(3)},\kappa_{R}^{(3)},\kappa_{L}^{(3)},b^{(3)},a^{(3)})

with

l\in C^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})

and a
\sigma^{\prime}\in C^{2}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))
.
Then we have
a^{(1)}(g)=a^{(2)}(g)=a^{(3)}(g)=:a(g)

and

\displaystyle\kappa_{R}^{(3)}(g,h)=d_{a}^{1}(l+m)(g,h)+\kappa_{R}^{(1)}(g,h),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\kappa_{L}^{(3)}(g,h)=d^{1}_{a}\left(b^{(3)}-b^{(1)}-(l+m)\right)(g,h)+\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h).

(2.14)

Note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(-1\right)^{\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h)\cdot m^{a(gh)}(k)}\left(-1\right)^{\left(b^{(2)}(g)-b^{(1)}(g)-m(g)\right)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{(2)}\right)^{a(g)}(h,k)}\\
&\left(-1\right)^{\kappa_{L}^{(2)}(g,h)\cdot l^{a(gh)}(k)}\left(-1\right)^{\left(b^{(3)}(g)-b^{(2)}(g)-l(g)\right)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{(3)}\right)^{a(g)}(h,k)}\\
&=\left(-1\right)^{\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h)\cdot(l+m)^{a(gh)}(k)}\left(-1\right)^{\left(b^{(3)}(g)-b^{(1)}(g)-(l+m)(g)\right)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{(3)}\right)^{a(g)}(h,k)}\\
&(-1)^{d^{1}_{a}(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)}-m)(g,h)\cdot l^{a(gh)}(k)+(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)}-m)(g)\cdot d_{a}^{1}l^{a(g)}(h,k)}\\
&=\left(-1\right)^{\kappa_{L}^{(1)}(g,h)\cdot(l+m)^{a(gh)}(k)}\left(-1\right)^{\left(b^{(3)}(g)-b^{(1)}(g)-(l+m)(g)\right)\cdot\left(\kappa_{R}^{(3)}\right)^{a(g)}(h,k)}\\
&d^{2}_{a}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(g,h,k)\end{split}

(2.15)

with
\sigma^{\prime\prime}(g,h):=(-1)^{(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)}-m)(g)\cdot l^{a(g)}(h)}
.
Here we used Lemma
2.1
in the last equation.
Setting
\tilde{\sigma}:=\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
,
we get

\displaystyle(c^{(1)},\kappa_{R}^{(1)},\kappa_{L}^{(1)},b^{(1)},a^{(1)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(c^{(3)},\kappa_{R}^{(3)},\kappa_{L}^{(3)},b^{(3)},a^{(3)})

with
l+m
and
\tilde{\sigma}
.

\square

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
(c,\kappa_{L},\kappa_{R},b,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)
,
set

\displaystyle\tilde{c}(g,h,k):=(-1)^{b(g)\cdot\kappa_{R}^{a(g)}(h,k)}c(g,h,k).

(2.16)

Then we have
(\tilde{c},\kappa_{R},\kappa_{R},0,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)

and
(c,\kappa_{L},\kappa_{R},b,a)\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(\tilde{c},\kappa_{R},\kappa_{R},0,a)
.

Setting

\displaystyle x(g,h,k):=(-1)^{b(g)\cdot\kappa_{R}^{a(g)}(h,k)},

(2.17)

one can check

\displaystyle d_{a}^{3}x(g,h,k,f)=(-1)^{d^{1}_{a}b(g,h)\cdot\kappa_{R}^{a(gh)}(k,f)},

(2.18)

using the fact that
d^{2}_{a}\kappa_{R}=0
.
From this, we have

\displaystyle d^{3}_{a}\tilde{c}(g,h,k,f)=d_{a}^{3}x(g,h,k,f)d_{a}^{3}c(g,h,k,f)=(-1)^{\left(d^{1}_{a}b(g,h)+\kappa_{L}(g,h)\right)\cdot\kappa_{R}^{a(gh)}(k,f)}=(-1)^{\kappa_{R}(g,h)\cdot\kappa_{R}^{a(gh)}(k,f)}.

(2.19)

Combining this and

\displaystyle d^{1}_{a}0(g,h)=0=\kappa_{R}(g,h)+\kappa_{R}(g,h),\quad d^{2}_{a}\kappa_{R}(g,h,k)=0

(2.20)

in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, we obtain
(\tilde{c},\kappa_{R},\kappa_{R},0,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)
.
Checking
(c,\kappa_{L},\kappa_{R},b,a)\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(\tilde{c},\kappa_{R},\kappa_{R},0,a)
. is immediate.

\square

Hence to consider
\mathcal{PD}(G)
, it suffices to think of the following.

Definition 2.5
.

We set

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}_{0}(G)=\left\{(c,\kappa,a)\mid(c,\kappa,\kappa,0,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}(G)\right\}.

(2.21)

We introduce the equivalence relation

\sim_{\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}_{0}(G)

by

\displaystyle(c^{(1)},\kappa^{(1)},a^{(1)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)}(c^{(2)},\kappa^{(2)},a^{(2)})\quad\text{if}\quad(c^{(1)},\kappa^{(1)},\kappa^{(1)},0,a^{(1)})\sim_{\mathcal{PD}(G)}(c^{(2)},\kappa^{(2)},\kappa^{(2)},0,a^{(2)}).

(2.22)

We denote the equivalence classes by
\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)
.
We also denote by
[(c,\kappa,a)]_{\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)}

the equivalence class containing
(c,\kappa,a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{PD}}_{0}(G)
.

The main theorem of this paper is the following.

Theorem 2.6
.

There is a
\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)
-valued index on
{\mathcal{P}}_{SL\beta}
, which is an invariant of the classification

\sim_{\beta}
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{SL\beta}
.

The proof follows the strategy of
[
O4
]
for the quantum spin system case.(See reviews and videos in
[
O2
]
[
O3
]
[
O5
]
.)
We consider the action of
\beta_{g}^{U}

on our ground state
\omega_{\Phi}
of
\Phi
in SPT-phase i.e.,

\omega_{\Phi}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}
.
Due to the fact that
\Phi
is in the SPT-phase,

\omega_{\Phi}
can be written as
\omega_{\Phi}=\omega^{(0)}\alpha

with some quasi-local automorphism
\alpha
, which does not create
the long range entanglement.
From this and that
\omega_{\Phi}
is
\beta_{g}
-invariant,
we see that the effective excitation caused by
\beta_{g}^{U}

on
\omega_{\Phi}
is localized around the
x
-axis.
In particular,
\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\beta_{g}^{U}\alpha^{-1}

satisfies the split property with respect to the cut
H_{L}-H_{R}
.
Namely, it is quasi-equivalent to a state of the form
\varphi_{L}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{R}
,
with homogeneous states
\varphi_{L},\varphi_{R}
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{L}}

{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}
.
The difference from the quantum spin case
[
O4
]

and our Fermionic case is that in Fermionic case when a state satisfies the split property,
there are two possibilities.
If the restriction of
\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\beta_{g}^{U}\alpha^{-1}
to
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}
is a factor state,
then it is equivalent to the state of the form
\omega^{(0)}\circ\left(\eta_{gL}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{gR}\right)
,
with some graded automorphisms
\eta_{gL}
,
\eta_{gR}

on
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{L}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}
localized around
x
-axis.
If the restriction is not a factor state, then it is equivalent to the state of the form

\omega^{(1)}\circ\left(\eta_{gL}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{gR}\right)
, with some other
reference state
\omega^{(1)}
.
This state
\omega^{(1)}
is of the form
\omega^{(0)}\circ\tau
,
with some automorphism
\tau
on the
x
-axis representing the space translation on the
x
-axis.
This dichotomy gives us the index taking value in
H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
.
Some combination of automorphisms
\eta_{gL}
and
\eta_{gR}
is implementable by a unitary in the GNS representation of
\omega_{\Phi}

and it
give us some indices in
C^{3}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))
(see Lemma
5.6
)
, just like in the quantum spin case. The
C^{2}(G,{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1))
part represents
if this unitary is even or odd.
Of course they are not independent to each other that
result in the complication of our index in
\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)
.
Without the doubled structure like
{\mathrm{U}}(1)\oplus{\mathrm{U}}(1)
,
result coincides with the one predicted in
[
BM
]

[
WG
]
.
For the moment, we do not know how to remove it. It comes from the fact that

\omega\circ\tau
and
\omega\circ\tau^{-1}

are not equivalent in general.
It might be possible to remove it under some symmetry.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In the analysis, the automorphic equivalence gets important.
It is explained in section
3
.
The
\eta_{g,L},\eta_{g,R}
above are given from the classification of
pure states satisfying the split property, analogous to that of
[
O1
]
.
Its Fermionic version is given in section
4
.
The
\mathcal{PD}_{0}(G)
-valued index is derived in section
5
.
In section
6
, we show it is actually the invariant of our classification.
Section
7
gives some convenient property of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}},{\mathfrak{C}}\right)
which we use in the analysis.
Notations from Appendix
A
are used freely.
Some facts about graded von Neumann algebras are collected/ proven in Appendix
B
.

3
Automorphic Equivalence

A very important fact about gapped ground state phases
is the automorphic equivalence
[
BMNS
]

[
NSY2
]
,

[
MO
]
,
which started as Hastings adiabatic Lemma
[
HW
]
.

First we introduce a class of paths of interactions.
A norm-continuous interaction on
{\mathcal{A}}
defined on an interval
[0,1]

is a map

\Phi:{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\times[0,1]\to{\mathcal{A}}
such that

(i)

for any
t\in[0,1]
,
\Phi(\cdot,t):{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to{\mathcal{A}}

is an even interaction, and

(ii)

for any
Z\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, the map

\Phi(Z,\cdot):[0,1]\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Z}

is piecewise norm-continuous.

Let
F
be an
F
-function on
({{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}},\mathrm{d})
.
(See
[
NSY2
]
or
[
O4
]
Appendix C for the definition of
F
-functions.)
We denote by
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{F}([0,1])
the set of all
norm continuous interactions
\Phi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
defined on an interval
[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\Phi\right\|\right|_{F}:=\sup_{x,y\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\frac{1}{F\left({\mathrm{d}}(x,y)\right)}\sum_{Z\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}},Z\ni x,y}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left(\left\|\Phi(Z;t)\right\|\right)<\infty.

(3.1)

From
\Psi\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{F}([0,1])
, we can construct
a path of automorphisms
\tau^{\Psi}_{t,s}

following
the same argument as quantum spin case

[
BSP
,
NSY1
,
BO
]
.
We can consider analogous path of interactions on

\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, which we denote by
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{F,\Gamma}([0,1])

For a subset
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}):=\left\{\alpha\mid\alpha=\tau_{s,t}^{\Psi},\;\text{for some}\;\Psi\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{F,\Gamma}([0,1]),\;F:F\text{-function}\;s,t\in[0,1]\right\},\\
&\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits_{\beta}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}):=\left\{\alpha\mid\alpha=\tau_{s,t}^{\Psi},\;\text{for some}\;\beta\text{-invariant}\Psi\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{F,\Gamma}([0,1]),\;\;F:F\text{-function}\;s,t\in[0,1]\right\}.\end{split}

(3.2)

They form subgroups of
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma})
.
Note from the proof of Theorem 4.7
[
AM
]

that for the conditional expectation
\Pi_{\Gamma}
, we have

\Pi_{\Gamma}(a\hat{\otimes}b)=0

for any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
and
b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma^{c}}^{(1)}
.
Using this fact, just by following the argument in
[
MO
]
,
we can show the following Fermionic version of automorphic equivalence.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
and
\omega_{\Phi_{0}}
,
\omega_{\Phi_{1}}

be their unique gapped ground states.
Suppose that

\Phi_{0}\sim\Phi_{1}
holds, via a path
\Phi:[0,1]\to{\mathcal{P}}_{UG}
.
Then there exists some

\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
such that

\omega_{\Phi_{1}}=\omega_{\Phi_{0}}\circ\alpha
.
If

\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{UG\beta}
and

\Phi\sim_{\beta}\Phi_{0}
,
we may take
\alpha
from

\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits_{\beta}({\mathcal{A}})
.

Because of the theorem,
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
plays an important role for us, and the ground states
we consider belong to the following set.

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{SPT}}\nolimits:=\left\{\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\mid\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}),\quad\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\circ\beta_{g}=\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha.\right\}.

(3.3)

For each
\omega\in\mathop{\mathrm{SPT}}\nolimits
, by definition, the set

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{EAut}}\nolimits(\omega)=\left\{\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})\mid\omega=\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\right\}

(3.4)

is non-empty.

From the fact they are given by local interactions, automorphisms in
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})

satisfy nice properties. We list up such properties for the rest of this section.
Most of them can be proven using the same argument as that
of quantum spin case
[
O4
]

combined with the property of conditional expectations
\Pi_{\Gamma}

mentioned above, and we omit the proof.

For
0<\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
, we set

\displaystyle C_{\theta}:=\left\{(x,y)\mid|y|\leq\tan\theta\cdot|x|\right\}.

(3.5)

For
0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
, we use a notation

{\mathcal{C}}_{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]}:=C_{\theta_{2}}\setminus C_{\theta_{1}}
and

{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,\theta_{1}]}:=C_{\theta_{1}}
.

We also set

\displaystyle c_{L}:=-5,\quad c_{R}:=5.

(3.6)

An automorphism
\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
satisfies a factorization property.
It basically says that we can split
\alpha
into two along any cut of the system
modulo some error terms localized around the boundary.
For example, if we cut the system along the
y
-axis, we obtain the following:
for any
0<\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
,
there are

\displaystyle\alpha_{L}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{L}}\right),\quad\alpha_{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}\right),\quad\Upsilon\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\left(C_{\theta}\right)^{c}}\right)

(3.7)

decomposing
\alpha
as

\displaystyle\alpha=({\rm{inner}})\circ\left(\alpha_{L}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{R}\right)\circ\Upsilon.

(3.8)

If we cut the system along the
x
-axis, we have the following:
there are

\displaystyle\alpha_{U}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{U}^{1}}),\quad\alpha_{D}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{D}^{-1}}),\quad\Xi_{L}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\theta}\cap H_{L}}\right),\quad\Xi_{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\theta}\cap H_{R}}\right)

(3.9)

such that

\displaystyle\alpha=\left(\alpha_{U}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{D}\right)\circ\left(\Xi_{L}\hat{\otimes}\Xi_{R}\right)({\rm{inner}}).

(3.10)

Hence for
\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
,
and
0<\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
, the following sets are non-empty.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{D}}^{V}(\alpha,\theta):=\left\{(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R},\Upsilon)\middle|\begin{gathered}\alpha_{L}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{L}}),\quad\alpha_{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{R}}),\quad\Upsilon\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\theta}^{c}}\right)\\
\text{such that}\quad\alpha=\left(\alpha_{L}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{R}\right)\Upsilon\circ({\rm{inner}})\end{gathered}\right\},\\
&{\mathcal{D}}^{H}(\alpha,\theta):=\left\{(\alpha_{U},\alpha_{D},\Xi_{L},\Xi_{R})\middle|\begin{gathered}\alpha_{U}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{U}^{1}}),\quad\alpha_{D}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{D}^{-1}}),\\
\Xi_{L}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\theta}\cap H_{L}}\right)\quad\Xi_{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\theta}\cap H_{R}}\right)\\
\text{such that}\quad\alpha=\left(\alpha_{U}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{D}\right)\circ\left(\Xi_{L}\hat{\otimes}\Xi_{R}\right)({\rm{inner}})\end{gathered}\right\}.\end{split}

(3.11)

We can consider finer factorization:
for each

\displaystyle 0<\theta_{0.8}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{1.2}<\theta_{1.8}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{2.2}<\theta_{2.8}<\theta_{3}<\theta_{3.2}<\frac{\pi}{2},

(3.12)

\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\alpha=({\rm{inner}})\circ\left(\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}]}\otimes\alpha_{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]}\otimes\alpha_{(\theta_{2},\theta_{3}]}\otimes\alpha_{(\theta_{3},\frac{\pi}{2}]}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{(\theta_{0.8},\theta_{1.2}]}\otimes\alpha_{(\theta_{1.8},\theta_{2.2}]}\otimes\alpha_{(\theta_{2.8},\theta_{3.2}]}\right)

(3.13)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\alpha_{X}:=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\sigma=L,R,\zeta=D,U}\alpha_{X,\sigma,\zeta},\quad\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}]}:=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\sigma=L,R}\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}],\sigma},\quad\alpha_{(\theta_{3},\frac{\pi}{2}]}:=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\zeta=D,U}\alpha_{(\theta_{3},\frac{\pi}{2}],\zeta}\\
&\alpha_{X,\sigma,\zeta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{X}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}\cap H_{\zeta}}\right),\quad\alpha_{X,\sigma}:=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\zeta=U,D}\alpha_{X,\sigma,\zeta},\quad\alpha_{X,\zeta}:=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\sigma=L,R}\alpha_{X,\sigma,\zeta}\\
&\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}],\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{C_{[0,\theta_{1}]}}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}}\right),\quad\alpha_{(\theta_{3},\frac{\pi}{2}],\zeta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{(\theta_{3},\frac{\pi}{2}]}\cap H_{\zeta}}\right),\end{split}

(3.14)

for

\displaystyle X=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}],(\theta_{2},\theta_{3}],(\theta_{0.8},\theta_{1.2}],(\theta_{1.8},\theta_{2.2}],(\theta_{2.8},\theta_{3.2}],\quad\sigma=L,R,\quad\zeta=D,U.

(3.15)

In order to define our index, we introduce an automorphism localized along
x
-axis.
Let
v_{\tau}
be a unitary on
{\mathfrak{h}}=l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})

such that

\displaystyle v_{\tau}\delta_{(x,y)}:=\left\{\begin{gathered}\delta_{(x,y)},\quad y\neq 0\\
\delta_{(x+1,0)},\quad y=0\end{gathered}.\right.

Note that

v_{\tau}
commutes with the complex conjugation

{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Therefore, it defines an automorphism
\tau:=\Xi_{v_{\tau}}

on
{\mathcal{A}}

such that

\displaystyle\tau\left(B(f)\right):=B\left(v_{\tau}f\right),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{h}}.

(3.18)

Decompositions like above allow us to derive the following Lemma immediately.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\right)
, the following hold.

(i)

For any
0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{2}
and
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
there are automorphisms
\zeta_{\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\varphi}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}}\right)
,

\sigma=L,R

such that

\displaystyle\alpha\tau^{a}\alpha^{-1}=\tau^{a}\left(\zeta_{L}\hat{\otimes}\zeta_{R}\right)\circ({\rm{inner}}).

(3.19)

(ii)

For any
0<\varphi^{\prime}<\varphi^{\prime\prime}<\frac{\pi}{2}

and
X_{\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\varphi^{\prime}}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}}\right)
,
\sigma=L,R

there are automorphisms

\tilde{X}_{\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}}\right)
,
\sigma=L,R

such that

\displaystyle\alpha\left(X_{L}\hat{\otimes}X_{R}\right)\alpha^{-1}=\left(\tilde{X}_{L}\hat{\otimes}\tilde{X}_{R}\right)\circ({\rm{inner}}).

(3.20)

Definition 3.3
.

For any
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and
X\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)
and

a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
we denote by

X_{a}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{(\Lambda_{0}+a\bm{e}_{0})\cup\Lambda_{1}}\right)

the automorphism
such that

\tau_{a}X\tau_{a}^{-1}=X_{a}
.
Here we decomposed
\Lambda
as

\displaystyle\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}\dot{\cup}\Lambda_{1}:=\left(\Lambda\cap\left({\mathbb{Z}}\times\{0\}\right)\right)\dot{\cup}\left(\Lambda\cap\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus\left({\mathbb{Z}}\times\{0\}\right)\right)\right),

(3.21)

and set
\bm{e}_{0}:=(1,0)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

From Lemma
3.2
(i)
and
\omega^{(0)}\circ\tau^{2a}=\omega^{(0)}
,
we obtain the following:

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}})
and
0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{2}
,
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}

there are automorphisms
\xi_{\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{C_{\varphi}\cap H_{\sigma}^{c_{\sigma}}}\right)
,
\sigma=L,R

such that

\displaystyle\omega^{(0)}\alpha\circ\tau^{2a}\simeq\omega^{(0)}\alpha\left(\xi_{L}\hat{\otimes}\xi_{R}\right).

(3.22)

4
Split property of pure states on Self-dual-CAR-algebras

Having the automorphic equivalence

\omega_{\Phi}=\omega^{(0)}\alpha
with
\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}})
and
the
\beta_{g}
-invariance of
\omega_{\Phi}
,
we expect that the effective excitation caused by
\beta_{g}^{U}

on
\omega_{\Phi}
is localized around the
x
-axis.
It can be shown so, by observing that

\omega^{(0)}\circ\alpha\beta_{g}^{U}\alpha^{-1}

satisfies the split property (Definition
4.3
) with respect to the
H_{L}-H_{R}
cut.
In this section, as a preparation of our analysis,
we investigate the split property of pure states on self-dual CAR-algebras.
For Fermionic systems, the split property gives some dichotomy (Lemma
4.4
).
The main proposition of this section is Proposition
4.5
,
which states that two pure split states belonging to the same category of the dichotomy
can be connected by automorphisms of graded tensor product form.
We use definitions and facts from Appendix
A
and Appendix
B
freely.

We start by some basic fact we repeatedly use.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
be Hilbert spaces
with complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}
, respectively.
Let
\varphi,\omega
be
homogeneous pure states on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
.
Suppose that
\varphi
and
\omega
are equivalent.
Then their restrictions
\varphi|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)}
,

\omega|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)}

onto
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)

are quasi-equivalent.

We write
{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}:={\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
, for
i=1,2
.
Let
({\mathcal{H}}_{\varphi},\pi_{\varphi},\Omega_{\varphi})

be a GNS representation of
\varphi
.
Because
\varphi
is homogeneous,
there is a self-adjoint unitary
\Gamma_{\varphi}

on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varphi}
such that

\Gamma_{\varphi}\pi_{\varphi}(A)\Omega_{\varphi}=\pi_{\varphi}\circ\Theta(A)\Omega_{\varphi}
,

A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
,
for the grading operator
\Theta
on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
.
Let
p_{\varphi}
be an orthogonal projection onto the subspace

{\mathcal{K}}_{\varphi}:=\overline{\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)\Omega_{\varphi}}
.
Because
p_{\varphi}\in\pi_{\varphi}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime}
,

\rho_{\varphi}(A):=\pi_{\varphi}\left(A\right)p_{\varphi}
,
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}

defines a representation of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}

on
{\mathcal{K}}_{\varphi}
.
Then
({\mathcal{K}}_{\varphi},\rho_{\varphi},\Omega_{\varphi})

is a GNS representation of
\varphi|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}}
.
By the Kaplansky density theorem,
we have
\rho_{\varphi}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}p_{\varphi}
.
Let us consider the map

\tau_{\varphi}:\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\to\rho_{\varphi}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime\prime}
,
defined by
\tau_{\varphi}(x):=xp_{\varphi}
, for
x\in\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}
.
It is a
*
-homomorphism onto
\rho_{\varphi}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
We claim that
\tau_{\varphi}
is injective.
To see this, we note
p_{\varphi}
and
\Gamma_{\varphi}

commute because
{\mathcal{K}}_{\varphi}
is
\Gamma_{\varphi}
-invariant.
As a result, we see that

\ker\tau_{\varphi}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)\left(\ker\tau_{\varphi}\right)
.
Let
E
be the central projection
of
\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}
such that

\ker\tau_{\varphi}=\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}E
.
From the above observation, we see that

\displaystyle\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)(E)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)\left(\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)(E)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)\left(\ker\tau_{\varphi}\right)=\ker\tau_{\varphi}=\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}E.

(4.1)

From this, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{\varphi}\right)(E)=E
.
Hence we have

\displaystyle E\in Z\left(\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right)\cap\left(\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right)\subset\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{2}\right)^{\prime}=\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I},

(4.2)

because
\varphi
is pure.
Then we conclude
E=0
and
this proves the claim, and our
\tau_{\varphi}
is a
*
-isomorphism.

Hence for GNS representations
\pi_{\varphi}
,

\rho_{\varphi}
of
\varphi
,
\varphi|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}}
respectively,
threre is a
*
-isomorphism
\tau_{\varphi}:\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\to\rho_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}

such that
\tau_{\varphi}\circ\pi_{\varphi}(A)=\rho_{\varphi}(A)
,
for each
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
.
Similarly, for GNS representations
\pi_{\omega}
,

\rho_{\omega}
of
\omega
,
\omega|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}}
respectively,
threre is a
*
-isomorphism
\tau_{\omega}:\pi_{\omega}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\to\rho_{\omega}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}

such that
\tau_{\omega}\circ\pi_{\omega}(A)=\rho_{\omega}(A)
,
for each
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
.
Because
\varphi
and
\omega
are equivalent,
there is a
*
-isomorphism
\tau:\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}\to\pi_{\omega}\left({\mathfrak{A}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}

such that
\tau\circ\pi_{\varphi}(A)=\pi_{\omega}(A)
for

A\in{\mathfrak{A}}\left({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
.
Restricting this
\tau
to
\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}
,
we obtain
a
*
-isomorphism
\tau_{1}:\pi_{\varphi}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}\to\pi_{\omega}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\right)^{\prime\prime}

such that
\tau_{1}\circ\pi_{\varphi}(A)=\pi_{\omega}(A)

for
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
.
Then we see that
\tau_{\omega}\circ\tau_{1}\circ\tau_{\varphi}^{-1}

defines a
*
-isomorphism from
\rho_{\varphi}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime\prime}

onto
\rho_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{1})^{\prime\prime}

such that
\tau_{\omega}\circ\tau_{1}\circ\tau_{\varphi}^{-1}\circ\rho_{\varphi}(A)=\rho_{\omega}(A)
,
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
.

\square

We encounter the following situation as well.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}
be Hilbert spaces
with complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}
, respectively.
Let
\omega
be
a homogeneous pure states on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
,
and
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
homogeneous states on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)
,
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
respectively.
If
\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}
,
then we have
\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{1}

and
\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{2}
.

By the proof of Lemma
4.1
,
we have
\left.\pi_{\omega}\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\pi_{\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}}
.
(Note that
\left.\pi_{\omega}\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}
is the restriction of the GNS representation of
\omega

while
\pi_{\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}}
is the GNS representation of the restriction of
\omega
.)
Because of
\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}
,
we have
\left.\pi_{\omega}\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\pi_{\varphi_{2}}
.
Hence we have
\pi_{\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\pi_{\varphi_{2}}
.
The same for
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)
.
This proves the Lemma.

\square

The situation in the previous Lemma has a name.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}
be an infinite dimensional Hilbert space
with a complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
We say a homogeneous pure state
\omega
on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)

satisfies the split property if
there are homogeneous states
\varphi_{i}

on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
,
i=1,2

such that

\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}
.

The following is a refinement of the dichotomy introduced in
[
M4
]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}
be an infinite dimensional Hilbert space
with a complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}
, for
i={1},{2}
.
Let
\omega
be a homogeneous pure state on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)
,
satisfying the split property.
Let
\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2}
be
automorphisms on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)

such that
\Theta_{1}\left(B(f_{1}\oplus f_{2})\right)=B\left(-f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\right)
,

\Theta_{2}\left(B(f_{1}\oplus f_{2})\right)=B\left(f_{1}\oplus(-f_{2})\right)
,
for
f_{i}\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Then one of the following occurs.

(i)

We have
\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}\Theta_{2}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}
.
The state
\omega
has a GNS representation of the form

({\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2},\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2},\Omega)

with
({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i})
an irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})
for
i={1},{2}
.
There is a self-adjoint unitary
\Gamma_{i}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
implementing
\Theta_{i}
, i.e.,

\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(\Gamma_{i})\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}\Theta_{i}
for
i={1},{2}
.
Decomposing
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
with
{\mathcal{H}}_{i\pm}:=\frac{\mathbb{I}\pm\Gamma_{i}}{{2}}{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,
as
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{i+}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{i-}
,
we have
\left.\pi\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)}=\pi_{i+}\oplus\pi_{i-}

with
\pi_{i\pm}
mutually singular irreducible representations on
{\mathcal{H}}_{i\pm}

of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
.

(ii)

The states

\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}\Theta_{2}

and
\left.\omega\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}

are mutually singular.
The state
\omega
has a GNS representation of the form

({\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2},\pi,\Omega)
.
There are irreducible representations
\pi_{i}

of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
, on
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,

i={1},{2}
such that

\displaystyle\pi\left(a\hat{\otimes}b\right)=\pi_{1}(a)\otimes\pi_{2}(b)\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}},\quad a\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right),\quad b\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right).

(4.3)

We have

\displaystyle\pi\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{z}+{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}\right),\quad\pi\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{x}+{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}\right).

(4.4)

We also have
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\sigma_{y}\right)\circ\pi=\pi\Theta
.

If
\omega
satisfies (i) (resp. (ii)), then
\omega\circ\left(\eta_{1}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{2}\right)

also satisfies (i) (resp. (ii)),
for any
\eta_{1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{{1}},{\mathfrak{C}}_{{1}}\right)\right)
,

\eta_{2}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{{2}},{\mathfrak{C}}_{{2}}\right)\right)
.

Let
\varphi_{i}

be
homogeneous states on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
,
i=1,2

such that
\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\varphi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{2}
.
Let
\omega_{i}:=\omega|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})}
,
i=1,2
,
on
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})
be the restriction of
\omega
, which is homogeneous.
By Lemma
4.2
, we have
\varphi_{i}\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\omega_{i}
and obtain

\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\omega_{1}\hat{\otimes}\omega_{2}
.

Because
\omega
is a pure state,
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}

i=1,2
are central.
(See Appendix
A
for the notation.)
They are also balanced because self-dual algebra has a self-adjoint odd unitary.
Hence the von Neumann algebras
\pi_{\omega_{i}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}

i=1,2

which are
*
-isomorphic to them (from the proof of Lemma
4.1
) are also central and balanced.
By
\omega\sim_{\mathop{q.e.}}\omega_{1}\hat{\otimes}\omega_{2}
,

\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}

is
*
-isomorphic to
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right))^{\prime\prime}

hence is a type I factor.
From Lemma
B.2
,
we conclude that both of
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}

and
\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}
are type I.
From Lemma
B.1
,
they are either a type I factor or a direct sum of two type I factors.
If
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}
is a factor
but not
\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}
,
then by Lemma A.2 of
[
BO
]

Z\left(\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}\right)
has a self-adjoint odd unitary
b

while
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}
includes a even self-adjoint unitary
\theta_{1}

implementing the grading on
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}
.

\theta_{1}\hat{\otimes}b
belongs to the center of a factor

\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}
,
which contradicts to the fact that the latter algebra is a factor.
Hence if
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}
is a factor, then

\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}
is a factor as well.
Similarly, if
\pi_{\omega_{2}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}))^{\prime\prime}
is a factor
then
\pi_{\omega_{1}}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}))^{\prime\prime}
is a factor as well.
As a result, either (i) both of
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2
are type I factors
or (ii) both of
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2
are direct sum of
two type I factors.
Note from Lemma 6.23 of
[
EK
]
that

\omega|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)}

is pure.

(i)If both of
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2
are type I factors, then from Lemma 5.5 of
[
BO
]
and its proof,
the state
\omega
has a GNS representation of the form

({\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2},\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2},\Omega)

with
({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i})
an irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})
for
i={1},{2}
.
Because
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}
is a type I factor, there
is a self-adjoint unitary
\Gamma_{i}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
implementing
\Theta_{i}
, i.e.,

\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(\Gamma_{i})\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}\Theta_{i}
for
i={1},{2}
.
From this,
\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}
implements
\Theta
in
\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2}
.
Decomposing
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
with
{\mathcal{H}}_{i\pm}:=\frac{\mathbb{I}\pm\Gamma_{i}}{{2}}{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,
as
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{i+}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{i-}
,
we have
\left.\pi\right|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)}=\pi_{i+}\oplus\pi_{i-}

with
\pi_{i\pm}
mutually singular irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
.
This last property follows from Lemma 6.24 of
[
EK
]
.

Because
\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\Gamma_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2})=\left(\left(\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2}\right)\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}
is even with respect to

\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}\right)
,
we have
\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\Gamma_{2}\in\left(\left(\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2}\right)\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}\right)^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\omega\circ\Theta_{2}|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)^{(0)}}(\cdot)=\left\langle\left(\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\Gamma_{2}\right)\Omega,\left(\pi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\pi_{2}\right)|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)^{(0)}}(\cdot)\left(\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\Gamma_{2}\right)\Omega\right\rangle

(4.5)

is quasi-equivalent to
\omega|_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)^{(0)}}
.

(ii)If both of
\pi_{\omega}({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}))^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2
are summation of
two type I factors, then by Lemma
B.3
,
the state
\omega
has a GNS representation of the form

({\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2},\pi,\Omega)
.
There are irreducible representations
\pi_{i}

of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{i},{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\right)
, on
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,

i={1},{2}
such that

\displaystyle\pi\left(a\hat{\otimes}b\right)=\pi_{1}(a)\otimes\pi_{2}(b)\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}},\quad a\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right),\quad b\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right).

(4.6)

We have

\displaystyle\pi\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{z}+{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}\right),\quad\pi\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{x}+{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}\right).

(4.7)

We also have
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\sigma_{y}\right)\circ\pi=\pi\Theta
.
Set
\Gamma_{2}:=\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\sigma_{z}
.
Note that
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{2}\right)\circ\pi=\pi\Theta_{2}

and

\displaystyle\pi\left(\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}+{\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{y}\right).

(4.8)

Hence the center of
\pi\left(\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}\right)^{\prime\prime}

is
{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\left({\mathbb{C}}r_{+}+{\mathbb{C}}r_{-}\right)

with
r_{\pm}:=\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}\pm\sigma_{y}}{2}
,
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(\Gamma_{2}\right)
flips
r_{+}
and
r_{-}
.
From this,
\pi_{0,\pm}:=\left.\pi\right|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}(\cdot)r_{\pm}

defines mutually singular irreducible representations of
\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}
.
Because
\omega|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}

is pure, there is some
\zeta=\pm
such that
\Omega=r_{\zeta}\Omega
.
Note that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}\Omega=\Gamma_{2}r_{\zeta}\Omega=\Gamma_{2}r_{\zeta}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{2}\Omega=r_{-\zeta}\Gamma_{2}\Omega.

(4.9)

From these, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\omega|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}=\left\langle\Omega,\pi_{0,\zeta}\left(\cdot\right)\Omega\right\rangle,\\
&\omega|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}\circ\Theta_{2}=\left\langle\Omega,\left.\pi\right|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}\circ\Theta_{2}\left(\cdot\right)\Omega\right\rangle\\
&=\left\langle\Gamma_{2}\Omega,\left.\pi\right|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}\left(\cdot\right)\Gamma_{2}\Omega\right\rangle=\left\langle\Gamma_{2}\Omega,\pi_{0,-\zeta}\left(\cdot\right)\Gamma_{2}\Omega\right\rangle,\end{split}

(4.10)

Because
\pi_{0,\pm}
are mutually singular,

\omega|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}

and
\omega|_{\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{2},{\mathfrak{C}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{2}\right)\right)^{(0)}}\circ\Theta_{2}

are disjoint.

The last statement comes from the fact
that
\pi_{\omega}\circ\left(\eta_{1}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{2}\right)

is a GNS representation of
\omega\circ\left(\eta_{1}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{2}\right)

and

\pi_{\omega}\circ\left(\eta_{1}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{2}\right)\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{{2}},{\mathfrak{C}}_{{2}}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{\omega}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{{2}},{\mathfrak{C}}_{{2}}\right)\right)^{\prime\prime},

where the right hand side is a factor if and only if
the left hand side is.

\square

Here is the main Proposition of this section.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\Lambda_{\sigma}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,
\sigma=L,R
be mutually disjoint
infinite subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
Set

{\mathfrak{K}}_{\sigma}:={\mathfrak{h}}_{\Lambda_{\sigma}}

with the complex conjugation
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\sigma}:={\mathfrak{C}}_{\Lambda_{\sigma}}
, for
\sigma=L,R
.
Let
\omega_{0},\omega_{1}
be homogeneous pure states on

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{L}\oplus{\mathfrak{K}}_{R},{\mathfrak{C}}_{L}\oplus{\mathfrak{C}}_{R}\right)
,
satisfying the split property.
Note from Lemma
4.4
, either (i) or (ii) of Lemma
4.4
occurs.
If

(a)

(i) occurs for both of
\omega_{0}
,
\omega_{1}
, or

(b)

(ii) occurs for both of
\omega_{0}
,
\omega_{1}
,

then there are automorphisms
\eta_{\sigma}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}\left({\mathfrak{K}}_{\sigma},{\mathfrak{C}}_{\sigma}\right)\right)

\sigma=L,R

satisfying

\displaystyle\omega_{1}\simeq\omega_{0}\left(\eta_{L}\hat{\otimes}\eta_{R}\right).

(4.11)

Furthermore, if none of (a), (b) occurs,
then
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{0}
are not quasi-equivalent.

The proof is a deformation of that of
[
O1
]
.
As we are considering non-twisted crossed product here, it is even simpler than the case of
[
O1
]
.
For the rest of this section,
\Lambda
indicates an infinite subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
We recall notations from
[
O1
]
adapted to our current setting.
An irreducible covariant representation of

\Sigma_{\Lambda}:=({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda},\Theta_{\Lambda})

is a triple
({\mathcal{H}},\pi,\Gamma)

where
({\mathcal{H}},\pi)
is an irreducible representation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}

and
\Gamma
a self-adjoint unitary on
{\mathcal{H}}

satisfying
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\Gamma\circ\pi=\pi\circ\Theta_{\Lambda}
.

Let
C({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})
be
the linear space of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda}}
-valued functions
on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
We equip
C({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})
with a product and
*
-operation as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}*f_{2}(h):=\sum_{g\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}f_{1}(g)\cdot\Theta_{\Lambda}^{g}\left(f_{2}(g^{-1}h)\right),\quad h\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}},

(4.12)

\displaystyle f^{*}(h):=\Theta^{g}_{\Lambda}\left(h\right)\left(f(h^{-1})^{*}\right),\quad h\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}},

(4.13)

for
f_{1},f_{2},f\in C({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})
.
The linear space
C({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})
which is a
*
-algebra
with these operations is denoted by
C(\Sigma_{\Lambda})
.

For a covariant representation
({\mathcal{H}},\pi,\Gamma)
of

\Sigma_{\Lambda}
, we may introduce a
*
-representation

({\mathcal{H}},\pi\times\Gamma)
of

C(\Sigma_{\Lambda})
by

\displaystyle\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(f):=\pi\left(f(0)\right)+\pi\left(f(1)\right)\Gamma,\quad f\in C(\Sigma_{\Lambda}).

(4.14)

The full twisted crossed product of
\Sigma_{\Lambda}
, denoted
C^{*}(\Sigma_{\Lambda})

is the completion of
C(\Sigma_{\Lambda})
with respect to the norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{u}:=\sup\left\{\left\|\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(f)\right\|\mid(\pi,\Gamma):\text{covariant representation}\right\},\quad f\in C(\Sigma_{\Lambda}).

(4.15)

Because
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is finite, we actually have
C(\Sigma_{\Lambda})=C^{*}(\Sigma_{\Lambda})
.
It also coincides with the reduced crossed product
C_{r}^{*}(\Sigma_{\Lambda})

and simple because
\Theta
is properly outer
[
E
]
.
As
A_{\Lambda}
is unital, we have unitaries
\lambda_{1}\in C^{*}(\Sigma_{\Lambda})
,
such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}a\lambda_{1}^{*}=\Theta_{\Lambda}\left(a\right),\quad a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}.

(4.16)

For the rest of this section, we use the following notations.

Notation 4.1
.

For each finite subset
\Lambda_{0}
of
\Lambda
,
let
\left\{e_{IJ}^{(\Lambda_{0})}\right\}_{I,J}
be a system of matrix units spanning

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}}
with

\Theta(e_{IJ}^{(\Lambda_{0})})=(-1)^{|I|+|J|}e_{IJ}^{(\Lambda_{0})}
.
Because
d
is even,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is even-dimensional and
our
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}}={\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{S}DC}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\Lambda_{0}})
is
isomorphic to a finite dimensional CAR-algebra.
Therefore, such system of matrix units exists.
Fix some
I_{0}
.
Set

{\mathcal{G}}_{\Lambda_{0}}:=\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e_{II_{0}}^{(\Lambda_{0})},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda_{1}e_{II_{0}}^{(\Lambda_{0})}\right\}_{I}
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
,
we fix
\delta_{2,a}(\varepsilon)>0
from Lemma B.4 of
[
O1
]
.

For any finite subset
{\mathcal{F}}
of
\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)_{1}
and

\varepsilon>0
,
there exists a finite subset
\Lambda(\varepsilon,{\mathcal{F}})
of
\Lambda

such that

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\left\|a-b\right\|\mid b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda(\varepsilon,{\mathcal{F}})}\right\}<\frac{\varepsilon}{16},

(4.17)

for all
a\in{\mathcal{F}}
.
For each finite subset
{\mathcal{F}}
of
\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)_{1}
and

\varepsilon>0
, we fix such
\Lambda(\varepsilon,{\mathcal{F}})
.

First we prepare a proposition needed for case (a) of Proposition
4.5
.
For an irreducible covariant representation
({\mathcal{H}},\pi,\Gamma)
of

({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda},\Theta_{\Lambda})
,
we set

With a bit abuse of notation, we call

.
We show the following Proposition.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i},\Gamma_{i})
,
i=0,1
be
irreducible covariant representations of

\Sigma_{\Lambda}
.
Let
({\mathcal{H}}_{i\pm},\pi_{i\pm})

({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i},\Gamma_{i})
,
and
\xi_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i+}
a unit vector.
Define a state
\omega_{i}
on

by

\displaystyle\omega_{i}:=\left\langle\tilde{\xi}_{i},\pi_{i}(\cdot)\tilde{\xi}_{i}\right\rangle

(4.19)

with

.
Then there is an automorphism
\eta\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits^{(0)}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)

such that

\omega_{1}=\omega_{0}\circ\eta
.

The point of the Proposition is that we can take

graded.

which relies on the techniques developed

-algebra theory
[

]

[
F
]
.

We just sketch the proof below.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i},\Gamma_{i})
,
i=0,1
be
irreducible covariant representations of

\Sigma_{\Lambda}:=\Sigma_{\Lambda}
.
Let
({\mathcal{H}}_{i\pm},\pi_{i\pm})

be the decomposition associated to
({\mathcal{H}}_{i},\pi_{i},\Gamma_{i})
,
and
\xi_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i+}
a unit vector.
With
\tilde{\xi}_{i}:=\xi_{i}\oplus 0\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,
let
\hat{\omega}_{i}
be pure states on
C^{*}(\Sigma)
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{i}:=\left\langle\tilde{\xi}_{i},\left(\pi_{i}\times\Gamma_{i}\right)(\cdot)\tilde{\xi}_{i}\right\rangle.

(4.20)

Then for any finite set
{\mathcal{F}}\subset C^{*}(\Sigma)
,
\varepsilon>0
,
there is a self-adjoint element

such that

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\omega}_{0}(a)-\hat{\omega}_{1}\circ\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(e^{ih})(a)\right|<\varepsilon,\quad\text{for all}\quad a\in{\mathcal{F}}.

(4.21)

This is a special case of Lemma 4.5
[
O1
]
.

\square

Lemma 4.8
.

For any finite subset
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
and

\varepsilon>0
,
there exists

\tilde{\delta}_{28}(\epsilon,\Lambda_{0})>0

satisfying the followings:
For any irreducible covariant representation
({\mathcal{H}},\pi,\Gamma)
of
\Sigma_{\Lambda}

and unit vectors
\xi,\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}

satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(x^{*})\xi\perp\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(y^{*})\eta,\\
&\left|\left\langle\xi,\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(xy^{*})\xi\right\rangle-\left\langle\eta,\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(xy^{*})\eta\right\rangle\right|<\tilde{\delta}_{28}(\epsilon,\Lambda_{0}),\\
&\text{for any}\quad x,y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\Lambda_{0}},\end{split}

(4.22)

there exists an even positive element
\hat{h}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\setminus{\Lambda_{0}},1}

such that

\displaystyle\left\|e^{i\pi\pi(\hat{h})}\xi-\eta\right\|<\frac{\delta_{2,a}(\frac{\epsilon}{8})}{4\sqrt{2}}.

(4.23)

With
\delta_{3,a}(\varepsilon,n)
given in Lemma B.6
[
O1
]
,
we set
\tilde{\delta}_{28}(\epsilon,\Lambda_{0}):=\delta_{3,a}(\epsilon,2^{d|\Lambda_{0}|+1})>0
.
Suppose that

({\mathcal{H}},\pi,\Gamma)
,
\xi,\eta

satisfy the condition (
4.22
) for this
\tilde{\delta}_{28}(\epsilon,\Lambda_{0})
.
Then by Lemma B.4 of
[
O1
]
,
there exists
h\in\left(C^{*}(\Sigma_{\Lambda})\right)_{+,1}

such that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(\bar{h})\left(\xi+\eta\right)\right\|,\;\;\left\|\left(\mathbb{I}-\left(\pi\times\Gamma\right)(\bar{h})\right)\left(\xi-\eta\right)\right\|<\frac{\delta_{2,a}(\frac{\epsilon}{8})e^{-\pi}}{4\sqrt{2}},

(4.24)

for

\displaystyle\bar{h}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma=0,1}\sum_{I}\lambda_{1}^{\sigma}e_{II_{0}}^{(\Lambda_{0})}he_{I_{0}I}^{(\Lambda_{0})}\left(\lambda_{1}^{\sigma}\right)^{*}\in C^{*}(\Sigma).

(4.25)

By the definition, we see that
\bar{h}

commute with
\lambda_{1}
and elements in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
From Lemma 5.4 of
[
BO
]
,
there are irreducible covariant representations
({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\pi_{1},\Gamma_{1})
,
({\mathcal{H}}_{2},\pi_{2},\Gamma_{2})

of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{0}}

and a unitary
